# FirebladeECS benchmark comparison

Jump to: [How to read this report](#how-to-read-this-report) · [Glossary](#glossary) · [Methods](#methods)

- Baseline: `1.4.3` (c85607bfe6893549bc7d058d36a4d662e8834eda)
- Inputs: 20 result files, 2 refs, 3 series (ref, component model, backend)
- Replicates per ref: 1.4.3: 10 of 10; main: 10 of 10
- Selection filter: `.*`
- Every replicate is an independent process; the replicate is the unit of analysis. `median` is the median over replicates of each replicate's median per iteration. `CI low` and `CI high` bound the 95% distribution-free confidence interval of that median (exact coverage in the methods section). `replicates` is the number of replicates behind the value, `iterations` the median number of measurements per replicate (time-based sampling, so it differs between series by design).
- Non-baseline series add, for wall clock only, `speed (×)` = baseline ÷ series (above 1 is faster) with its exact 95% CI, `change (%)` = (1 ÷ speed − 1) × 100, `q` (Benjamini–Hochberg adjusted p of an exact Mann–Whitney test across every wall-clock comparison of this report) and a `verdict`: `faster`/`slower` need q < 5% and a CI entirely outside the ±5% zone, `equivalent` needs the CI entirely inside it. Other metrics are descriptive only.
- Every unit is in its column header and applies to the whole column of that table. Values keep at most the 3 significant digits the package measured. Anything missing or not comparable is `unavailable`, and a metric the package did not record (throughput of an iteration of about 2 s or longer) is `n/a` (hover for the reason), never zero. `n/a (k replicates)` means the statistic needs more replicates.
- Plots show the wall-clock median per iteration on log-log axes with CI error bars and a light within-run IQR band (spread of the measurements inside a run, not uncertainty); entity count 0 cannot sit on a log axis and appears in the tables only.

## Summary

Wall clock, `speed (×)` against the paired baseline series (above 1 is faster) with its exact 95% CI and the verdict (δ = 5%, q across all comparisons). The last block of each series is the largest entity count for which the comparison exists (its count in the `entities` column).

## How to read this report

Short version: look at `speed (×)` (above 1 the series is faster than the baseline), check that its CI is narrow, and read the `verdict`. Words in `code` style are defined in the [glossary](#glossary).

### 1. What a confidence interval is

- Every number here is measured with noise, so a single speed such as 2.0× could have come out 1.9× or 2.1× in another session. The **confidence interval (CI)** is the range that, with 95% confidence (nominal; the exact coverage is in the methods), contains the true value.
- `CI low` and `CI high` are its two ends. Read the pair as "the truth is very likely between these".
- **Narrow** CI: the estimate is precise, you can trust the digits you see. **Wide** CI: the data cannot pin the value down; look for more replicates before drawing a conclusion.
- **Example from this report:** `entity.lookup.none` at 1,024 entities, `main (value, archetype)`: speed 1.37×, CI 1.36–1.39× → the true speedup is very likely between 1.36× and 1.39×.
- Narrowest CI in this report: `entity.lookup.two` at 16 entities, `main (value, archetype)`, ±0.038% around the speed (speed 2.67×, CI 2.666–2.668×). Widest: `entity.lookup.one` at 1 entities, `main (value, archetype)`, ±33% (speed 1.51×, CI 1.01–2.00×).

### 2. How the verdict is decided

A verdict is given only for wall clock and only when both refs have at least 2 replicates. We call differences smaller than ±5% "the same" (the **equivalence zone**, speeds from 1/1.05 to 1.05).
- `faster` / `slower`: the test is significant (`q` < 5%) **and** the whole CI is outside the zone (above 1.05, or below 1/1.05).
- `equivalent`: the whole CI is inside the zone, so any real difference is smaller than ±5%.
- `inconclusive`: everything else. A significant test is not enough: if the CI still overlaps the zone, the effect could be smaller than 5% and not worth acting on, so the honest answer is "cannot tell yet".
- `n/a (k replicates)`: a side has fewer than 2 replicates.
- **In this report:** 753 faster, 17 slower, 35 equivalent, 25 inconclusive, 0 n/a.
- Example `faster`: `component.add` at 1,024 entities, `main (value, archetype)`: speed 3.60×, CI 3.56–3.65×, q <0.001.
- Example `slower`: none among the cells with entities and a non-zero CI width.
- Example `equivalent`: none among the cells with entities and a non-zero CI width.

### 3. Error bars are not the light band

- **Error bars** (and the CI columns) show the uncertainty of the *median across runs*: how much it would move if you repeated the whole measurement. More replicates make them shorter.
- The **light band** shows the *spread of single iterations inside one run* (p25 to p75, the IQR). It says how steady one iteration is, and more replicates do not make it narrower.
- They answer different questions: a workload can be very jittery (wide band) and still have a precisely known median (short bars), or the other way round.
- **Example from this report:** `entity.lookup.none` at 1,024 entities, `main (value, archetype)` (wall clock): median 13.2 μs; error bar (CI of the median over 10 replicates) 13.0–13.3 μs; light band (IQR of single iterations) 13.1–13.3 μs.

### 4. Reading the plots

- **Log axes** (the time plots): equal distances mean equal *ratios*, not equal differences. Going from 1 to 10 looks the same as 10 to 100. A straight line is a power law and its slope is the growth rate: slope 1 means time grows in proportion to the entity count; a flatter line grows slower.
- **Per-entity plot**: time divided by the entity count. A flat line means the cost of one entity does not change with scale.
- **Linear plot**: absolute time on plain axes, including entity count 0. It shows what the log plot hides: how big the differences are in absolute terms at large scale.
- **Relative plot**: speed against the baseline. The dotted line at 1× is "same as the baseline"; the shaded band is the ±5% equivalence zone; the ribbon is the CI of the speed. A series whose ribbon lies entirely above the zone is faster, entirely below slower.
- **Markers**: in the time plots a *hollow* marker has no CI at that point. In the relative plot a *filled* marker is a `faster` or `slower` verdict, a hollow one anything else.
- **Error bars** are drawn in a darker (light theme) or lighter (dark theme) shade of the series colour; the plot caption states the largest CI shown ("largest CI: ±x%"), so tiny bars are explained rather than missing.

### 5. What `q` is

- `p` is the chance of seeing a difference at least this large if the refs were truly the same. A report makes hundreds of comparisons, and at a 5% threshold about one in twenty *equal* comparisons would look significant by luck alone.
- `q` is `p` adjusted for that (Benjamini–Hochberg): flagging everything with `q` < 5% keeps the expected share of false alarms among the flagged rows at about 5%. Judge a row by `q`, never by its raw `p`.
- **Example from this report:** 830 wall-clock comparisons were tested, so unadjusted about 42 would pass p < 5% by chance alone. The strongest, `component.add` at 1 entities, `main (value, archetype)`, has p 0.001 and q <0.001.

### 6. Replicates (k) and interleaving

- A **replicate** is one complete, independent run of a ref in its own process. `k` is how many a ref has. Iterations within one run share their warm-up, memory layout and machine state, so they are not independent evidence; the replicates are.
- More replicates give narrower CIs and make verdicts possible: an interval of the median needs k ≥ 6, a speed interval about 4 per side, and the test can only reach q < 5% with enough of them (k = 10 is the practical minimum).
- Runs are **interleaved**: each round runs every ref once, in a shuffled order, instead of all runs of one ref and then all of the other. A slow minute on the machine (thermal throttling, a background job) then hits all refs alike instead of one, and the drift check can notice it.
- **In this report:** replicates per ref: 1.4.3: 10 of 10; main: 10 of 10.

## Glossary

Every abbreviation and term of this report, in plain language. Column units are stated in each table header.

- **Baseline**: The ref every other ref is compared with (the first input, or the one given with `--baseline`). Its speed is 1× by definition.
- **Ref**: A Git tag, branch or commit of FirebladeECS that was built and measured.
- **Series**: One line in a plot and one block of table columns: a ref with one component model and one backend.
- **Component model**: How components are stored: `class` (reference types, the 1.x design) or `value` (value types, the 2.0 design).
- **Backend**: The storage strategy of a ref: `archetype` packs entities with the same components together, `sparseSet` keeps one sparse set per component, `default` is a ref with a single storage.
- **Entities**: How many entities the workload runs with; the x axis of the plots and the rows of the tables.
- **Iteration**: One timed execution of the workload. `iterations` is the typical (median over replicates) number of iterations measured per replicate; it differs between series because sampling is time-based.
- **Replicate, k**: One independent process run of a ref. `k` is how many replicates a ref has. The replicate is the unit of analysis: iterations of one run are not independent samples. A round runs every ref once; replicates are interleaved over rounds.
- **Median**: The middle value: half of the values are smaller, half larger. Per replicate it is the median of its iterations; the table and the plot line show the median over replicates of those.
- **Mean**: The average of the iterations of a replicate (median over replicates). Descriptive only; outliers pull it.
- **Percentiles p0 … p100**: `pN` is the value below which N% of the iterations fall (median over replicates). p50 is the median, p25 and p75 the quartiles. For metrics where larger is better (throughput) p0 is the largest value.
- **IQR, within-run spread**: Interquartile range: the spread from p25 to p75 of the iterations inside one run (median over replicates). It shows how much single iterations vary, not how sure we are about the median, and is drawn as a light band in the plots.
- **CI, confidence interval**: A range that, with the stated probability (95% nominal; the exact coverage of each interval is printed under Methods, never less than 95%), contains the true value of what it estimates. Narrow means precise, wide means uncertain. Error bars show it for medians, the ribbon for speeds.
- **CI low, CI high**: The lower and upper bound of the CI. Same unit as the column they belong to; `(×)` columns are ratios.
- **Largest CI**: In a plot legend, the biggest half-width of any error bar or ribbon shown, relative to its centre; it tells whether bars too small to see are really that small.
- **Speed (×)**: baseline ÷ series, so above 1 the series is faster and below 1 slower than the baseline (2× means twice as fast). Estimated with Hodges–Lehmann.
- **Change (%)**: The same effect as a percentage of the baseline time: (1 ÷ speed − 1) × 100. Negative means the series needs less time.
- **Hodges–Lehmann, HL**: A robust estimate of the typical ratio between two sets of replicate medians: the median of all pairwise ratios (baseline ÷ series). Few outliers can not move it much.
- **Mann–Whitney U**: A rank test that asks whether replicate medians of one ref tend to be larger than those of the other, without assuming a bell-shaped distribution. Computed exactly (no approximation).
- **p**: The probability of seeing a difference at least this large if the two refs were in truth equal. Small p suggests a real difference; it does not say how large.
- **q, BH**: p adjusted by the Benjamini–Hochberg procedure (BH) for the many comparisons in this report, so that among everything flagged at q < 5% only about 5% are expected to be false alarms. Use q, not p, to judge a row.
- **δ, equivalence zone**: The band of differences we call "the same": ±5%, that is speeds from 1/1.05 to 1.05. Shaded in the relative plot.
- **Verdict**: The classification of a wall-clock comparison; one of `faster`, `slower`, `equivalent`, `inconclusive` or `n/a`.
- **faster, slower**: q < 5% and the whole CI of the speed lies outside the ±5% zone: above 1.05 for faster, below 1/1.05 for slower.
- **equivalent**: The whole CI of the speed lies inside the ±5% zone: any difference is smaller than we care about.
- **inconclusive**: Neither: the data allow a difference larger than the zone as well as none (for example the CI crosses the zone edge, or q is not below 5%).
- **n/a (k replicates)**: Not available. `n/a (k replicates)` means a side has fewer than 2 replicates, too few for an interval, test or verdict. `n/a` in a throughput cell means the benchmark package did not record the metric: Benchmark 1.38.1 records throughput only above 0 operations per second, so an iteration of about 2 s or longer has none (hover for the reason stated by the result file). `unavailable` marks a missing or non-comparable value (hover for the reason: not measured, no measurements, incompatible units, zero median, no estimate). It is never zero.
- **Kendall τ, drift**: Kendall's rank correlation between round and replicate median. A significant τ means results moved over the course of the session (warm-up, thermal throttling, a background job); the Methods section lists the cells where that happened.
- **Wall clock**: Elapsed real time per iteration. The only metric that is plotted and tested.
- **CPU total**: CPU time (user plus system) used per iteration; can exceed wall clock when several threads run.
- **Throughput**: Iterations per second; larger is better. Units: ops/s, Kops/s (thousands), Mops/s (millions).
- **Peak RSS**: Resident set size: the largest amount of physical memory the process held (kB, MB, GB).
- **Malloc count**: How many heap allocations an iteration made; fewer is better.
- **Per entity, total**: `per-entity` divides the time by the entity count (cost of one entity); `total` is the time of the whole iteration.
- **Log and linear axes**: On a log axis equal distances mean equal ratios and a straight line is a power law whose slope is the growth rate; log2 doubles per step, log10 multiplies by ten. A linear axis shows absolute differences and also entity count 0.
- **Markers**: In the time plots a hollow marker has no CI at that point. In the relative plot a filled marker is a significant `faster` or `slower`, a hollow one anything else.
- **Units**: ns, μs, ms, s are nanoseconds, microseconds, milliseconds and seconds; B, kB, MB, GB bytes; K, M, G thousand, million and billion (counts); each column states its unit in the header.

## Methods

- **Protocol:** each ref is built once and run k = 10 times as independent processes (1.4.3: 10 of 10; main: 10 of 10). Replicates run in interleaved rounds: each round runs every ref once, in an order shuffled by the recorded seed (seed 20261031; batch `B29346A9-FFEA-4808-A674-E0B2AB7FDB46`). The suite settings are identical for all refs.
- **Per replicate:** median, p25, p75 and mean of the iterations, read from the stored histogram. **Per series and cell:** the centre is the median of the replicate medians; the error bar is the distribution-free order-statistic confidence interval of that median, [x(l), x(k−l+1)] with the largest l such that P(Binomial(k, ½) ≤ l−1) ≤ 0.025 (omitted and marked when k < 6); the light band is the median over replicates of each replicate's p25 and p75.
- **Coverage:** median CI k = 10: 97.9% (nominal ≥ 95%, exact). Speed CI 10 vs 10 replicates: 95.7%.
- **Effect:** `speed (×)` = baseline ÷ series, estimated by Hodges–Lehmann on the log replicate medians (exp of the median of all pairwise log differences) with the exact Mann–Whitney-based confidence interval of those differences. `change (%)` = (1 ÷ speed − 1) × 100.
- **Test:** exact two-sided Mann–Whitney U on the replicate medians (permutation distribution conditional on ties). **Multiplicity:** Benjamini–Hochberg across all 830 wall-clock comparisons with a p-value in this report; `q` is the adjusted value and the threshold is q < 5%.
- **Verdict:** δ = 5%, symmetric on the log scale, so the equivalence zone is [1/1.05, 1.05]. `faster`: q < 5% and CI low > 1.05; `slower`: q < 5% and CI high < 1/1.05; `equivalent`: the CI lies inside the zone; otherwise `inconclusive`; `n/a (k replicates)` when either side has fewer than 2. Only wall clock is tested; all other metrics are descriptive.
- **Drift:** Kendall's τ between round and replicate median per series and wall-clock cell, exact p (0 of 1245 use a normal approximation because of ties), Benjamini–Hochberg adjusted across 1245 tests. 0 cells show a significant trend (q < 5%).
- **Limits:** each ref is built once, so effects of code layout between builds are not sampled. Conclusions hold for the recorded host and toolchain only. The Benchmark plugin checks and relinks the build before each replicate, identically for all refs. With k = 5 per side the smallest attainable p (0.0079) cannot pass Benjamini–Hochberg over several hundred tests; k = 10 is the practical minimum for meaningful verdicts.

| `component.add` | adapted: component one is removed from the whole batch before timing; upstream pauses to remove per entity; per-iteration untimed setup counts against the package's 0.5 s wall budget, so measured time and sample count are below upstream's 0.5 s of measured time, especially at large scales | 1.4.3, main |
| `component.remove-add` | adapted: payload is a single Int vs upstream float structs; every set allocates a new class instance on 1.x vs a value copy on value revisions; nexus teardown is outside the timed region; every pass returns the entities to their initial occupancy; component types are declared with @Component (stored static componentIndex) on value revisions and by plain class conformance on 1.x; the generic CompatBox stays plain on every revision; state is built once and kept across iterations as upstream; sampling is time-based (0.5 s, no warmup) so pass count and world state can differ by revision, as upstream | 1.4.3, main |
| `entity.create.empty` | adapted: nexus teardown is outside the timed region; upstream times registry teardown; per-iteration untimed setup counts against the package's 0.5 s wall budget, so measured time and sample count are below upstream's 0.5 s of measured time, especially at large scales | 1.4.3, main |

## Unsupported workloads

Unsupported workloads are not measured and not approximated.

| Workload | Reason | Refs |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `entity.create.empty-bulk` | no native bulk operation; createEntities(count:) loops over per-entity creation and Family.destroyMembers destroys entity by entity | 1.4.3, main |
| `entity.create.minimal-bulk` | no native bulk operation; createEntities(count:) loops over per-entity creation and Family.destroyMembers destroys entity by entity | 1.4.3, main |
| `entity.destroy.minimal-bulk` | no native bulk operation; createEntities(count:) loops over per-entity creation and Family.destroyMembers destroys entity by entity | 1.4.3, main |
| `event.dispatch.enqueue-only.mixed` | no arbitrary-event dispatcher or queue API | 1.4.3, main |
| `event.dispatch.enqueue-update.mixed` | no arbitrary-event dispatcher or queue API | 1.4.3, main |
| `event.dispatch.trigger-update.mixed` | no arbitrary-event dispatcher or queue API | 1.4.3, main |
| `event.dispatch.update-only.mixed` | no arbitrary-event dispatcher or queue API | 1.4.3, main |
| `event.observer.emit-update.mixed` | no arbitrary-event observer or emit API; Nexus events are structural only | 1.4.3, main |
| `event.observer.enqueue-update.mixed` | no arbitrary-event observer or emit API; Nexus events are structural only | 1.4.3, main |
